The Levellers would dislike its authority.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-319" href="#sec-04-fn-319" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-319" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-95"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="95" data-folio="83" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/eastindiatradein00khanuoft/page/n94_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 83">[p. 83]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Though Courteen's son had lost all his money, and was now a fugitive, his place was taken by a group of West India merchants, headed by Martin Noell and Maurice Thomson.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-320" href="#sec-04-fn-320" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-320" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> As the trade to the East Indies was now practically open, and any adventurous merchant might charter a ship and trade there, the Assada merchants had fully taken advantage of the freedom and had sent several ships. They were, therefore, opposed to the grant of a charter that would prohibit them from venturing on the Indian Ocean, and would empower the East India Company to confiscate their goods. They were, moreover, desirous of establishing plantations at Assada, as that would, in their opinion, provide them with a centre of lucrative commerce with East Africa, Asia, and even America. Moreover, they wanted to extend the sphere of the Company's activity to Guinea, China, and Japan. Nor did they regard the management of the Company as thoroughly satisfactory. 'A Joint Stock has produced neither profit nor encouragement to the Adventurers.' The liberty of trade followed as a corollary from the grant of political liberty 'and they considered that the prohibition was absolutely against the national liberties'.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-321" href="#sec-04-fn-321" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-321" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> They, therefore, advocated 'a free, well regulated trade' rather than a Joint Stock. This advocacy of the freedom of trade was not novel, and as early as the reign of Elizabeth Cecil had denounced those who would take away the Englishman's 'natural right of trade'.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-322" href="#sec-04-fn-322" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-322" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">What was entirely novel, however, was the change in the opinion on commerce in general, and the East India trade in particular. The State now assumes the functions which were exercised with so much success by the central government under Charles II. Nor could Noell have failed to mould Cromwell's opinion on the subject.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-323" href="#sec-04-fn-323" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-323" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> The Company could hardly reject all the proposals put forward by the Assada merchants. Its trade had declined; it had lost many ships in the war; and it had, finally, some of those merchants on its own Committee. It was, however, very suspicious of the ultimate success of the rash <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-96" data-page="96" data-folio="84" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/eastindiatradein00khanuoft/page/n95_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 84">[p. 84]</button> projects of Thomson, nor was it convinced that Assada could be made profitable to the adventurers.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-324" href="#sec-04-fn-324" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-324" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> 'It desired a settled factory as much as any,' but it rightly pointed out that its successful execution would require money, which it did not possess in abundance. It was prepared, however, to concede the other propositions, and an agreement was therefore arrived at.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-325" href="#sec-04-fn-325" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-325" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Under this agreement, the amount of stock to be raised was fixed at £300,000, and the question of the form of government for the future Company was left to the decision of the future shareholders. The plantation at Assada was to be proceeded with, while a settled, fortified habitation 'in India was to be established as soon as possible '.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-326" href="#sec-04-fn-326" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-326" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> The amalgamation of the two Companies facilitated the ratification of the agreement by the Parliament, and the Company was secured against further attacks by the resolution of Parliament that 'the East India Trade be carried on by one Company with one Joint Stock, and the management thereof be under a regulation in such a manner as the Parliament shall think fit '.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-327" href="#sec-04-fn-327" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-327" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> Though the subscription to the new Joint Stock did not bring in more than £200,000, it may be regarded as fairly satisfactory. The United Stock thus formed, in 1650, had been heavily subscribed by the Assada merchants, and they claimed a share in the management of the Company.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-328" href="#sec-04-fn-328" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-328" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup> It was now decided to appoint two sets of Committees, one for each body. The United Joint Stock chose thirteen of its members. One of them was Thomson.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-329" href="#sec-04-fn-329" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-329" role="doc-noteref">6</a></sup> Thomson's policy aimed at the complete transformation of the Company into a Regulated Company, planting colonies after the Dutch model, allowing every adventurer who desired to trade in the East, upon payment of a fee, and extending its commerce to Japan, China, and Guinea. He was backed up by many shareholders, and at the General Meeting held on 10th May 1654 considerable diversity of opinion was manifested on the subject. The Thomson party advocated the reorganization of the Company on a broader <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-97" data-page="97" data-folio="85" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/eastindiatradein00khanuoft/page/n96_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 85">[p. 85]</button> basis, though the Committee appointed reported in favour of the Joint-Stock system.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-330" href="#sec-04-fn-330" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-330" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Thomson, however, was not dismayed. The Company's petition to Cromwell, praying him to confirm the Company's charter and to prohibit interloping ships from trading to India, was replied to by the party in a series of petitions to the Council and the Protector.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-331" href="#sec-04-fn-331" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-331" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> His strength lay in the unification of the various grievances, and the utilization of the latter in his own interests. The Leveller, the city merchant excluded from the Company, the Puritan soldier who had fought against the King, would unite in condemning a Company that had been founded on a charter granted by a king, and that excluded every other English merchant from a share in the trade. A regulated trade would, on the other hand, encourage ‘industry and ingenuity, and afford latitude and scope for both, every one having the ordering of his own business’. It would, moreover, increase the number of traders, and would give opportunity to all to adventure their estate at any time. ‘This regulated way of trade will give some advantage over the Dutch, as going at less charge and having more opportunity to improve the industry of the managers in India.’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-332" href="#sec-04-fn-332" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-332" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> It was the Joint-Stock system that was the cause of the decline of trade. The lack of profit, the want of success, and the decay were all due to the exclusive monopoly of the East India Company.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The State was now regarded by both parties as the sole instrument whereby the East India trade could be effectively carried on. Robinson had suggested the nationalization of the East India trade. Under Cromwell the functions assigned to the State are widened. ‘Without the protection and countenance of the State’, says a petition of the East India Company, ‘no man can promise himself safety or profit in the India Trade.’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-333" href="#sec-04-fn-333" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-333" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> This is expressed in the most thoroughgoing manner by a writer of the time. The State was to undertake the management of the East India trade. The writer thought that ‘£200,000 would carry it’. If it was not carried by the State, but by the Company, the result would be that ‘these would serve the nation <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-98" data-page="98" data-folio="86" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/eastindiatradein00khanuoft/page/n97_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 86">[p. 86]</button> with what they please, at what price they please, and if they alone be allowed to trade in Spices, they can make what profit they like ’.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-334" href="#sec-04-fn-334" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-334" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> ‘ Without assistance from His Highness and the State, nothing can do any good to the trade.’</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This feature is common to nearly all the pamphlets of the period. The State is regarded as the means whereby the wishes of the authors can be realized. Perhaps the new ideas were nowhere better expressed than in Wylde’s tract on the East India trade.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-335" href="#sec-04-fn-335" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-335" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> It is headed a ‘ Remonstrance to the Lord Protector ’. Wylde urges the Protector to apply <em>lex talionis</em> to the Dutch, ‘ whom to turn out of those trades will be but what they have long laboured at, and had even now effected it, had not your Highness beaten them into better manners here at home ’. The Dutch were to be ‘ beaten into better manners ’. This was not all. The Portuguese, too, should be compelled to disgorge some of their ill-gotten wealth in the East. His Highness should acquire a ‘ commodious harbour in India ; and should secure the town of Dieu from the inhabitants thereof ’. The Great Mogul was to be offered an alliance, and he will ‘ willingly embrace an offer of aiding him by sea in his war against ’ the King of Bijapur. Nor should the Molucca and Banda islands be neglected, as the state of the Dutch garrisons there was little better than that of slaves. They would, asserted Wylde, be only too glad to come under Cromwell’s protection. Wylde knew that the East India Company was totally unable to carry such a comprehensive scheme into effect. He therefore urged the Protector to raise a National Stock of a million sterling. All classes were to subscribe to it, and all the counties were to provide their quota. The East Indian commodities were to be allowed entry into the plantations, and ‘ Calicoes, and diverse sorts of stuffs, made in India, and very proper for the hot countries ’, were to be imported into Barbadoes.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This comprehensive scheme was not carried into effect, because the Protector was busy elsewhere. That the remonstrance of Wylde had some effect is evident from the following document : ‘ Having with all respect and thankfulness considered His Highness’s intention to endeavour the settlement of a national interest <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-99" data-page="99" data-folio="87" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/eastindiatradein00khanuoft/page/n98_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 87">[p. 87]</button> in India, the East India Company propose, as places most convenient, the town of Bassein, with the port Bone-Bay.'<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-336" href="#sec-04-fn-336" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-336" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Moreover, a letter in Thurloe<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-337" href="#sec-04-fn-337" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-337" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> says that the news of the Protector's resolution to dissolve the East India Company 'had produced consternation among the merchants at Amsterdam'.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Company's answer to its critics was not different, so far as the protection of the State was concerned. Both parties were unanimous upon that point. The decay of trade, which the Thomson party attributed to the Joint-Stock system, was asserted by the Company to be due to the want of that system. The Company claimed that it had steadied the prices and done away with 'cut-throat' competition.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-338" href="#sec-04-fn-338" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-338" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> The results of the open trade had by no means been happy. Cloth, which was bought formerly for 10<em>s.</em>, cost 16<em>s.</em> 4<em>d.</em> at the time. The ships of private merchants were competing frantically with one another in the Eastern ports, with the result that they were being forced to sell and buy at whatever rates the Indian merchants demanded.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-339" href="#sec-04-fn-339" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-339" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> A letter from Madras<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-340" href="#sec-04-fn-340" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-340" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup> says that in 1656 the price of coarse saltpetre in Bengal was more than double that of the refined sort in 1655, owing to the competition of English ships, 'striving to outvy one another both in price and presents'.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-341" href="#sec-04-fn-341" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-341" role="doc-noteref">6</a></sup> This account is confirmed by the author of <em>Britannia Languens.</em> 'Whilst the trade was open, in the years 54, 55, and 56, our merchants sold the Indian commodities so low that they furnished more parts of Europe then since we have done, nay, Holland and Amsterdam itself.'<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-342" href="#sec-04-fn-342" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-342" role="doc-noteref">7</a></sup> Pollexfen asserted—in 1680—that during the time when the trade was open, the prices of the East India commodities went down considerably.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Company, therefore, were completely justified in pointing out the consequences of the open trade in the East. They asserted that if the trade were laid open, 'there will be many sellers, all anxious to dispose their merchandise, thereby cheapening the price and raising that of Indian commodities'. Again, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-100" data-page="100" data-folio="88" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/eastindiatradein00khanuoft/page/n99_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 88">[p. 88]</button> if it were not united, it would be ‘subject to the affronts and oppressions of other nations, to which, having to do with fourteen sovereign princes and with the Dutch and Portuguese as competitors, it is very liable’.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-343" href="#sec-04-fn-343" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-343" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Perhaps the most remarkable defence was that made by ‘Samuel Lamb’ of London.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-344" href="#sec-04-fn-344" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-344" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> The pamphlet is characterized by the breadth of view which is such a distinguishing trait of the period. The writer pleads for many reforms, and is specially enthusiastic in his description of the Dutch banks. He gives many reasons for the successes of the Dutch. The main reason, however, is the policy of their statesmen. Many of them are merchants in present trade, ‘or have been bred so in their minorities’.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-345" href="#sec-04-fn-345" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-345" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> He praises their good customs, ‘their care and their vigilancy’. ‘The chief and considerable way, by which they have wrought themselves what they are, are banks.’ Lamb then discusses the advantages that England would derive from the institution of banks and Merchants’ Courts. This, however, would not remove the Dutch menace. The English merchants must be prepared to strike hard. Otherwise, ‘the studious industry of our neighbours in Holland will soon overbalance us, if not timely prevented’. ‘As the Spaniard aims to get the universal monarchy of Christendom, so the Hollander the universal trade, not only of Christendom, but of all the known world.’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-346" href="#sec-04-fn-346" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-346" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> This was apparent from the condition of the East India Company. The East India Company had employed in that time 15,000 tons of shipping, ‘but they were so decayed, through the undermining of the Dutch, that they had hardly one ship remaining at their giving over’. Hence, the Company ought to be encouraged by the Protector and given a charter. The Joint Stock was the only organization that is suitable for the purpose. In this pamphlet we see how the various causes interacted on one another. ‘The nation which hath most warlike shipping and mariners will command in chief at sea; and he that commands the sea may command in trade; and he that commands the trade will have the most money.’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-347" href="#sec-04-fn-347" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-347" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The policy of Cromwell has been differently stated. Hunter <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-101" data-page="101" data-folio="89" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/eastindiatradein00khanuoft/page/n100_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 89">[p. 89]</button> thought Cromwell took a personal interest in the East India Company. But the information we possess does not justify us in holding this view. He seems to have regarded it as a purely private concern, and he was not inclined to meddle with any private business.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-348" href="#sec-04-fn-348" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-348" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> He was no doubt busy at the time (1651), and the Company’s tactless petitions could hardly be entertained favourably by a stranger. He was a friend of Martin Noell,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-349" href="#sec-04-fn-349" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-349" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> and was no doubt guided by his policy. Noell himself was a busy West Indian merchant, and had taken a leading part in the opposition to the Company.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-350" href="#sec-04-fn-350" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-350" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> He was a determined opponent of the Joint-Stock system, and the Protector no doubt favoured the idea of the establishment of a national interest. The authorities cited above seem to confirm the view. He may well have disliked the monopoly exercised by the Company. He certainly granted commissions to ‘many private ships’ with the consequence, as the Company complained in a petition to the Protector in 1656, ‘that the price of native goods in India had been raised 40 or 50 per cent., while that of English ships had been lowered’.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-351" href="#sec-04-fn-351" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-351" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> There are numbers of references to the licences granted at the time to private merchants in the <em>Calendars of State Papers, Domestic Series.</em> We may conclude, therefore, that the Protector favoured the policy of Noell. His ‘astounding’ proposal to the Dutch Commissioners about the partition of Asia and America has been discussed by Dr. Gardiner.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-352" href="#sec-04-fn-352" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-352" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup> It shows that the Protector’s knowledge of those countries was, to say the least, very superficial.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Company was, however, in a critical situation at the time. Its trade had declined; interlopers were underselling English goods in the East, and carrying on an insecure trade by resorting to questionable devices. The markets were glutted with Eastern commodities and the owners were obliged to send the surplus to continental countries, including Holland, where the cheap rates at which the goods were offered depressed the shares of the Dutch East India Company.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-353" href="#sec-04-fn-353" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-353" role="doc-noteref">6</a></sup></p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-102"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="102" data-folio="90" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/eastindiatradein00khanuoft/page/n101_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 90">[p. 90]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Company had made several attempts to obtain a confirmation of their charter from the Protector. There was, however, no sign of the Protector and his Council coming to a decision regarding the trade. At last, on 14th October 1656, the Committees of the United Joint Stock resolved to propose to their shareholders that the Company’s privileges in the East should be sold for £14,000 to ‘some Englishmen’.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-354" href="#sec-04-fn-354" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-354" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> It was decided, however, to make one more effort, and a fresh petition was therefore presented on 20th October. It was referred by the Council to a Committee of eight of their members, with Colonel Philip Jones as chairman. This did not, however, settle the difficulty. The year 1656 wore on, and nothing was accomplished. The Company grew desperate and resolved, at a general meeting held in January 1657, to appoint a sale on 14th February of all their rights and properties in the Indies, and bills were ordered to be set up on the Exchange notifying their intention.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-355" href="#sec-04-fn-355" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-355" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> The threat had the desired effect. Even Noell fell into line with the Company’s policy, and a charter was granted in 1657.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-356" href="#sec-04-fn-356" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-356" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> ‘It confirmed all privileges and immunities granted by James and added fresh ones.’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-357" href="#sec-04-fn-357" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-357" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> Perhaps this is all that we are entitled to say about it, as it was suppressed by the Company immediately after the Restoration. It had, however, one important result. The vexed question of organization that had troubled the Company for more than ten years was at last settled. The Company was granted an exclusive monopoly of the trade in the East and was allowed to fortify and plant in any of its settlements. The grant had immediate effects. The money subscribed for a new stock ‘exceeded all expectation’. The total was £739,782. The amount subscribed shows the increasing popularity of the East India trade. It is, moreover, a testimony to the soundness of the Joint Stock organization in the seventeenth century. The people were not willing to invest considerably in an institution whose existence was threatened either by a domestic or by a foreign rival. The amount proved, however, to be more than the Company could immediately use with any prospect of profit. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-103" data-page="103" data-folio="91" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/eastindiatradein00khanuoft/page/n102_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 91">[p. 91]</button> Consequently, after the first two instalments, aggregating 25 per cent., had been collected, it was decided to reduce the next two payments to 12½ per cent. each; and the rest of the capital was never called in. The capital of the New General Stock was therefore a little under £370,000.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-358" href="#sec-04-fn-358" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-358" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Committees of the Company set to work to push the trade with vigour. In December 1657 it was agreed that the lease held by the Guinea Company should be transferred to the East India Company for £1,300. Numerous ships were sent out to the East.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-359" href="#sec-04-fn-359" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-359" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> The Company's sphere of activity was widened. Two English vessels reached Canton in safety, and returned to England in 1659.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This wonderful revival of the East India Company testified to the vigour of the Protector's foreign policy. There is no evidence to prove that he took a personal interest therein. The East India merchants do not seem to have been consulted in the course of his negotiations with the Dutch for peace. They remained in the background. They reaped, however, the benefits that accrued from the Treaty of Westminster, 5th April 1654. The treaty provided, among other things, that the Dutch were to punish those who had been responsible for the Massacre of Amboyna, and that all claims for losses prior to the outbreak of the war were to be referred to a Joint Anglo-Dutch Commission. The English Company's claim for £2,696,000 was no less fantastic than those of the Dutch Company, who claimed £2,920,000. The English Company was ultimately awarded £85,000, and was also given back Pularoon. The Company, however, was obliged to lend £50,000 to the State. On 26th October 1656 it was obliged to lend £10,000 to the Commissioners of Customs. The total amount which the State owed has been calculated by Mr. Foster<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-360" href="#sec-04-fn-360" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-360" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> at £46,000. There is no reason to believe that the money was ever repaid.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">But the disadvantages of these measures were more than counterbalanced by the vigour of the Protector's foreign policy. The Company had gained what Mun desired. Its most serious rivals had been totally defeated and its trade protected by the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-104" data-page="104" data-folio="92" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/eastindiatradein00khanuoft/page/n103_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 92">[p. 92]</button> State. Cromwell himself did not like the Dutch war,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-361" href="#sec-04-fn-361" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-361" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> yet he was obliged to carry it on for the maintenance of the material interests of the merchants. It was the merchants demanding redress for various grievances against the Dutch, grievances that had been accumulating for over half a century, that compelled him to fight the Dutch to the bitter end. The causes of the rivalry have already been mentioned.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-362" href="#sec-04-fn-362" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-362" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> The war would have been declared long ago, if a vigorous foreign policy had been possible at the time. It is for this reason that Cromwell may be called the founder of the mercantile system. It is this intimate connexion of the foreign policy of the Protector with, and its effect on, the maintenance of the East India trade that is so characteristic of the Cromwellian period. The Protector himself may not have been conscious of this.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-363" href="#sec-04-fn-363" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-363" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> He certainly disliked the Dutch because they preferred commercial advantages. Yet the bold outline of his policy is quite clear. By employing the total resources of a vigorous State against the Dutch in the interests of commerce and navigation, it facilitated the Protectionism of Charles II and the Colbertism of the Whigs.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-364" href="#sec-04-fn-364" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-364" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup></p></div><section class="apparatus-zone" aria-label="Footnotes"><h2 class="apparatus-heading">Critical Apparatus &amp; Notes</h2><ol class="apparatus-list"><li id="sec-04-fn-001" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-001" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This sketch of the East India trade is confined mainly to the commerce carried on by England with the East Indies. Barbosa, De Laet, Faria y Sousa, Maffeius, Major, Monserrate, Pyrard, Father Hosten, Maclagan, Yule, Varthema, Bernier, Sewell, Della Valle, Hawkins, Peter Mundy, Roe, Coryat, and John Marshall are known to all students of seventeenth century Indian history, and the writer has derived considerable help from the priceless information contained in their writings ; but his object in writing this essay is not so much to sketch the organization of Indian industry as to trace the course of English commerce with India, and estimate its effect on the economic and foreign policy of England. Hence, these writers occupy only a subordinate place in the development of this essay.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-002" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-002" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Monson's <em>Naval Tracts</em>, printed in Churchill's <em>Collection of Voyages</em>, vol. iii, p. 408.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-003" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-003" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Weare's <em>Cabot's Discovery of North America</em>, pp. 144–50.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-004" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-004" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Narratives of Voyages</em>, by Rundell; Sainsbury, vol. i, Introduction i–xxxiv, passim; <em>The Three Voyages of Frobisher</em>, by Collinson.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-005" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-005" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>History</em>, vol. i, p. 236.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-006" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-006" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Bruce, vol. i, pp. 136–9; Bruce, vol. ii, pp. 112–13.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-009" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-009" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Constitution and Finances of the Joint Stock Companies</em>, pp. 96–7.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-010" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-010" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">London's <em>Lord Have Mercy Upon Us</em>, in Somers' Tracts, vol. vii, p. 54.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-011" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-011" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sambrooke's <em>Report on the Progress of the East India Trade</em>, printed in Sainsbury, vol. ix, pp. 360–3.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-012" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-012" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">In Sainsbury, <em>ib</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-013" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-013" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">Court Book, vol. ii, 27th February 1607.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-014" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-014" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is not a single exhaustive treatise in English on the subject. George Edmundson’s article on the subject (<em>Cambridge Modern History</em>, vol. v, pp. 510–14) is very valuable, but it does not give us sufficient information. Motley’s <em>United Netherlands</em> contains acute remarks on the subject, and is indispensable (vol. iii, p. 652; vol. iv, pp. 148–51). Hunter’s <em>History of British India</em> (vol. i) has a valuable chapter on the subject. Professor Egerton’s chapter in the <em>Cambridge Modern History</em> (vol. v, chap. xxv, pp. 729–46) is useful and accurate. The most trustworthy information is, however, to be sought in the dispatches of the factors and Court Minutes of the English Company.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-015" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-015" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sainsbury, vol. i, no. 440; <em>Court Minutes</em>, Book II. 125–7; Shaw, <em>Charters, &amp;c.</em>, pp. 27–53.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-016" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-016" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">It is instructive to compare the amount given by Dudley Digges in his <em>Defence of Trade</em>, pp. 43–4, with those in Mun, vol. v, pp. 276–7.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-017" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-017" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">George Edmundson, op. cit.; Sainsbury, vol. ii, nos. 268, 463, 666; vol. iii, nos. 156, 267, 370; Bruce, vol. i, p. 155. Mus. 509. h. 4, 1686, pp. 53, 54.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-019" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-019" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ib. 522. m. 20, 1700, pp. 18–24.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-020" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-020" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ib. 509. h. 4, p. 54.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-021" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-021" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Dudley Digges, op. cit., p. 44.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-022" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-022" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Malynes, op. cit., p. 54.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-023" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-023" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mun, <em>Discourse of Trade</em>, Purchas, vol. v, pp. 268–9.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-024" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-024" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Digges, p. 43. Later on the Company, to avoid seizure of its merchandise by the State, sold its pepper at Genoa, and other Italian towns. See below. Sainsbury, vols. vii, viii, passim.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-025" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-025" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Venetian Calendar</em>, 1613-15, p. 160. Compare Piero Contarini's dispatch, in volume dealing with 1617-19. The number of articles desired by Chamberlain was seventy. The list in Macpherson, vol. ii, p. 131, is large enough. Compare p. 106. Mun's list is by far the most accurate and concise, pp. 265-6. Sainsbury, vols. i-v, passim. The references to spices in the Court Minutes, &amp;c., are so frequent that I have deliberately left them unnoticed. It would have considerably increased the bulk of this essay. Only the most important are given below.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-026" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-026" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See below.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-027" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-027" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mun, p. 266.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-028" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-028" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ib.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-029" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-029" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">The first mention of the name in the East India Company’s records occurs under date August 1602 : ‘Book Calicoes, bought in certain Junks near Socotra at 4<em>s.</em> a piece. They are worth here at 12<em>s.</em> a piece’ : Sainsbury, vol. i, nos. 309, pp. 135, 271. ‘Inquiry to be made how Calicoes and Pinlathoes will sell in England, that directions may be given to factors in the East Indies to buy and send them over’ : p. 317. ‘Indigo, Calicoes, &amp;c., the Chief Commodities in Surat’ : ib., p. 328, nos. 776, 792 ; vol. ii, p. 73, nos. 88, 608, 750 ; Mun, op. cit., pp. 265–80. Sainsbury’s <em>Court Minutes</em>, vols. vi to x, show the increasing popularity of the calicoes. There was a reduction in demand owing to the Civil War, the Interlopers, and the famine in India in 1630. After 1657 we find a noticeable change.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-030" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-030" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mun, op. cit., p. 272.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-031" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-031" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Court Minutes</em>, 22–25th Sept. 1619, Book IV ; Sainsbury, vol. ii, pp. 155–9. A very accurate account of the Persian trade.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-032" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-032" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sainsbury, vol. ii, p. 156.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-033" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-033" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Foster, op. cit. ; Sainsbury, vol. i, nos. 840–2, 859, 865, 921, 922 ; vol. ii, pp. 44, 45, 55, 57, nos. 403, &amp;c. ; Mun, op. cit., p. 269.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-034" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-034" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Lex Mercatoria</em>, p. 54.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-035" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-035" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ib., p. 54.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-036" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-036" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sainsbury, vol. iv, nos. 10, 12, 18, 25, 69, 110, 314, 315, pp. 172, 173; vol. vi, p. 140. 'Saltpetre, of which there are about 50 or 100 tons to be sold at £4. 10<em>s.</em> per cwt.' In February 1611 it was sold at £40 a cwt., pp. 149, 164. The price varied from £4 to £4. 10<em>s.</em>, pp. 221–2, 232, 253–5; vol. viii, pp. 325, 346, 350.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-037" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-037" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Scott, op. cit., p. 113.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-038" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-038" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Dictionarium Rusticum</em>, 1717. Compare H. Townshend, <em>Historical Collections</em>, p. 251, 1680.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-039" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-039" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">D'Ewes, <em>Journals</em>, p. 653, &amp;c. A very vivid account of the patents granted by the Queen.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-040" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-040" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sainsbury, vols. ii and iii, passim.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-041" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-041" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ib., vol. ii, p. 159, nos. 339, 753. See the price of wool on p. 65 of vol. iv, and nos. 230, 767, 803. They are quoted by Hunter, vol. i. The treaties of December 1605, 17th May 1606, 3rd July 1596, June 1607, in the Java MSS., show the leading features of the Dutch policy in the East. Mr. Edmundson, <em>Cambridge Modern History</em>, vol. v, p. 710, gives a clear, though necessarily brief, account of that monopoly. Compare also, p. 732, ib. Motley calls it 'a Mighty Monopoly', <em>United Netherlands</em>, vol. iii, pp. 651-2. This is, perhaps, the truest description of the Dutch Company.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-070" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-070" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Purchas, vol. ii, pp. 456–62.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-071" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-071" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ib., pp. 320–1.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-072" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-072" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ib., pp. 523–49.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-073" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-073" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ib., pp. 422–3.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-074" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-074" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Walter Peyton’s Second Voyage, Purchas, vol. iv, pp. 302–3.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-075" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-075" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Collection of Voyages and Travels</em>.  Printed by Thomas Astley, vol. i, p. 301. The Hollanders used every possible endeavour to debar the English from trade with Acheen, ‘but the more they sought, the less they prevailed’.  Nos. 671, 673.  ‘The island of Machian was offered to Sir Henry Middleton ; the inhabitants expected his return for three years, when they were forced to yield to the Flemings.’  No. 630.  William Adams’s letter from Japan, pp. 231–2.  The fifth volume of Purchas, pp. 1–232, is full of ‘Dutch force and fraud’, ‘their lying devices’, ‘their cruelties’, ‘the base dealings of the Dutch’, ‘their cunning tricks in Banda’.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-077" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-077" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Journals of Captain Pring (Purchas, vol. v, chap. vii), of Master Nathaniel Courthop, chap. ix, and of other travellers in the East, teem with details of the Anglo-Dutch rivalry in the East.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-078" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-078" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>English Industry</em>, vol. ii, p. 1.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-079" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-079" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Camden Society, <em>Letters of John Chamberlain</em>, edited by Sarah Williams, 1861, p. 12 et passim. anonymously in November 1608. It formed the twelfth chapter of his work, <em>De Iure Praedae</em>, which was written in 1604–5. The manuscript of this work, written when the author was only twenty-one years of age, was not discovered till 1864, and was published in 1868. Walker (<em>History of the Law of Nations</em>, pp. 278–83); Hall, <em>International Law</em> (6th edition), pp. 140–51, and Oppenheim, <em>International Law</em> (1905), pp. 300–8, have treated this subject lucidly. It is interesting to note that William Wellwood anticipated some of the arguments of Selden in his <em>Sea Law of Scotland</em> (Bodleian Library, Oxford), and an <em>Abridgement of all Sea Laws</em>, and subjected Grotius's book to keen criticism. He appeals to the Scriptures; quotes the Roman lawyers; and has frequent recourse to history to prove his points, and to demolish the foundations of Grotius's arguments. But he lacked the charm of his style, and the width of his intellect, and though the comparative failure of his book was due to the elaborate and eminent work of Selden, the faulty arrangement and the scholastic pedantries which disfigure his chapter on 'the Community and propriety of the Seas' contributed to their failure. (See also Fulton, <em>Sovereignty of the Seas</em>.)</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-129" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-129" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Walker's <em>History of the Law of Nations</em>, vol. i, pp. 278–83, 1899. The case of the <em>Louis</em>, decided by Lord Stowell, in 1817.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-130" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-130" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ib., p. 38, ed. Sainsbury, vol. i, nos. 854, 855, 860, 874, 976.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-215" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-215" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Winwood, Memorials</em>, vol. iii.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-216" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-216" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Court Book</em>, vol. iv, 3rd Jan. 1615, pp. 328-31.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-217" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-217" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Court Minutes</em>, Book III, pp. 419-22, 5th May 1615.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-218" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-218" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Court Minutes</em>, Book III, pp. 419-22, 28th June 1615.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-219" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-219" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Court Minutes, Court Book</em>, vol. iii, 28th June 1615, pp. 432-8; <em>Public Record Office, East Indies</em>, vol. i, no. 48; <em>Court Book</em>, 18th Aug. 1615, pp. 463-7.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-220" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-220" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sainsbury, vol. i, nos. 1004, 1006, 1023.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-221" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-221" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ib., vol. ii, pp. 384–6.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-222" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-222" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The whole volume of <em>State Papers, East Indies</em>, 1617–21, is full of them. So is Purchas, vol. v, op. cit.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-223" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-223" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Public Record Office, East Indies</em>, vol. i, no. 7; <em>Domestic, Jac. I</em>, vol. civ, no. 62, p. 607.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-224" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-224" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">James’s foreign policy has been exhaustively dealt with by Professor Gardiner in his first five volumes of the <em>History of England</em>. It is evident, however, that the Anglo-Dutch struggle in the East has not received the same attention that he devoted to James’s policy towards Spain and the Palatinate. Though he refers to the struggle in chap. xxvi, the account he gives of it is not completely satisfactory. Again, though he mentions the East India papers calendared by Sainsbury in the preface to vol. iii, it does not appear that all the voluminous material collected therein was thoroughly digested. He relies greatly upon the Add. MSS. 17677. The transcripts from the archives of the Hague are undoubtedly important; but the study of the original correspondence of the Company’s factors and the Court Minutes of the East India Company are of no less importance. I find him deficient in the latter.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-225" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-225" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">Chamberlain to Carleton, 30th Jan. 1619. Sainsbury, vol. ii, no. 572.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-226" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-226" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Doncaster to Carleton, 12th May 1619. Sainsbury, vol. ii, no. 665.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-227" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-227" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Court Book</em>, vol. iv, pp. 347–50, 21st May 1619.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-228" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-228" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Court Minutes</em>, vol. iv, pp. 345–7, 14–17th May 1619.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-229" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-229" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Viz. one-third of all the spices in those parts, and all the pepper at Bantam, <em>Court Minutes</em>, ib.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-230" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-230" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">Chamberlain to Carleton, 31st May 1619. Sainsbury, vol. iii, no. 114.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-245" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-245" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See next chapter.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-246" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-246" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Public Record Office, East Indies</em>, vol. ii, nos. 26, 28.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-247" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-247" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ib., nos. 27, 32.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-248" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-248" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See a copy of the Treaty in the <em>Calendars of State Papers, East Indies</em>, 1622–4, pp. 106–7.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-249" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-249" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sainsbury, vol. iii, no. 233. The Secretary of State himself told Carleton that ‘we had at last made an end and parted good friends, though with much loss and disadvantage to the English Company as was conceived’. Carleton’s dispatch of 5th Feb. 1623 shows that the Dutch were elated by the ‘favourable usage’ received from James. Sainsbury, vol. iii, no. 257.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-250" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-250" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">They have been exhaustively dealt with by Gardiner, in vols. iv and v of his <em>History of England</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-251" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-251" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>History</em>, vols. iv and v.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-252" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-252" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sainsbury, vol. iii, nos. 511, 524, 534, 574. Copies of the pamphlet published by the Company were presented to the Lords of the Council and the principal nobility. The Venetian Ambassador in England testified to the intensity of hatred against the Dutch, <em>Venetian Calendar</em>, vol. xviii. The pamphlet was dispersed in all parts of England, as well as in the Netherlands, ib., pp. 463, 660, 688. Copies were much asked after by Members of Parliament, ib. 544.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-253" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-253" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>East Indies</em>, 1622–4.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-254" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-254" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Court Minutes</em>, 22–24th Nov. 1624.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-255" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-255" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ib. 22–24th Nov. 1624.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-256" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-256" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sainsbury, vol. iv, pp. 503–8.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-257" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-257" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ib., nos. 640, 641.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-258" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-258" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ib., nos. 701, 706.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-259" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-259" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ib., no. 719.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-260" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-260" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>East Indies</em>, vol. iv, nos. 51 and 51. 1.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-261" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-261" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sainsbury, vols. v, vi. Political naturally led to social equality, and we are therefore quite prepared to find the ‘Anabaptists’ and Levellers as opponents of the monopoly of the East India Company. There is hardly any mention of the East India Company among the economic theories of the period. Yet what little there is seems to confirm the view. Many of the Cromwellian soldiers went out to India. Evelyn said that the refusal of some members to take the oath prescribed by the Charter was due to the ‘Anabaptists’, whose object was to be able to continue their private trade to the East.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-320" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-320" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See Andrews, <em>British Committee of Trade</em>, an account of Noell and Povey; Sainsbury, <em>Calendar of State Papers, West Indies, 1570-1660</em>.
